# Unfilter digest 01 — pregnancy and birth
Read on 2026-11-17. Scope: 1,317 quote records: 356 from voice/reddit-ur04.jsonl, 635 from voice/reddit-ur24.jsonl, and 326 from voice/reddit-ur40.jsonl. Read every quote, the pregnancy-birth README, the current findings’ structure and conclusions, and each ledger’s “What these posts add” section. This file is the digest; it does not rewrite the findings or declare the rest of the reread queue complete.

Counts throughout count quotes, never people. The share was selected because posts had previously been excluded; it is not a sample of prevalence. Several quotes can describe one experience or author. Where shown, speaker, stage and context labels come from the records; diagnoses, identities and ages are not newly inferred. Clinical claims, explanations of loss, medication beliefs, legal rules and reported institutional policies remain the speakers’ accounts.

Each numbered pattern states its relationship to an exact passage from the current findings and the change warranted by this share. Evidence blocks reproduce complete quote fields, preserving their spelling, punctuation, wording and internal whitespace. Index labels and analytical prose are interpretations, not quotations. Repetitive evidence is retained as further supporting ids; quotations reserved for the counter-examples are linked from the relevant pattern. Every money/request index entry has its full source quotation elsewhere in this digest.

## Changes the final rewrite needs to carry

- Give actual loss, pregnancy after loss, fetal diagnosis and decisions about continuation their own trajectories. Pregnancy count is not the number of living children, and continuation is not necessarily an intention to parent.
- Expand the partner account to include coercion in both directions, bodily and financial control, sexual/reproductive consent, supportive partners and fathers’ own distress or opposition.
- Describe why help may not be sought: custody/CPS fears, shame, prior dismissal, closed services, withheld results, cost, transport and childcare. A contact list or task list alone is insufficient.
- Keep prenatal crisis distinct from ordinary uncertainty and postpartum crisis distinct from an unprepared or unsupported household. Functioning outwardly does not establish that the parent is well.
- Add medication continuity, the mother’s treatment and function, medical-birth education, service-contract boundaries, feeding capacity and recovery beyond two weeks.
- Retain positive outcomes, relief, pride, good care, easy pregnancies and different preferences about information. None of the added spending establishes demand or willingness to pay for the proposed Mamivio guide.

## Patterns and their evidence

### 1. Loss involves labour, procedures, unfinished care and grief for a particular baby; a later baby does not erase it.

**Current findings:** §§2, 4–6, 9; add an explicit loss and post-loss-care trajectory.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The first weeks include physical incapacity, feeding pressure, conflicting instructions, loneliness, regret and delayed connection.”

**What should change:** The current arc still reads mainly as a route toward bringing a living newborn home. Add miscarriage, ectopic pregnancy, stillbirth, neonatal death, induction after loss, medical/surgical/expectant management, what follow-up remains, memorials and the possibility of ending attempts. Keep the reported events and uncertainty; these accounts do not establish causes of loss. The Badger record closing can end the pregnancy interface before the parent knows whether care has ended.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

The scan was booked in due to some pains I was having and was concerned about - nothing debilitating and definitely nothing I was worried about THAT much. I definitely didn’t think this would be the outcome. Unfortunately, on the day I thought I’d be seeing our baby for the first time, I was being told there would be no baby. I have been heartbroken. This was severely painful, emotionally and physically. The whole experience has been traumatic. I needed to share this story somewhere, tell people what happened, so it feels more real. Obviously, our friends and family who knew about the pregnancy know what has happened. But other than that, it’s like there was never anything there. Like the world has no idea there should be another baby in the world. I feel so sad and empty. All my plans and hopes are shattered. Just when I had begun to get excited. ### 2. After loss, gestational milestones, shopping and trying again carry meanings that pregnancy number cannot express.

**Current findings:** §§1–2, 6, 9–10, 16.5, 17.3–4.

**Relationship:** extends and contradicts an assumption.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Second-time content must assume she has a child to mind”

**What should change:** A subsequent pregnancy need not mean a living child or experience of later pregnancy. Record pregnancy count, living children and loss history separately. The prior loss supplies a week, anniversary, feared examination or decision about another attempt. Shopping can create hope or feel like inviting public grief; a buy-now/hold-off calendar alone misses both. Some celebrate a new positive or feel content after loss, and those accounts remain alongside dread.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

### 3. Parents cannot reliably turn bleeding, cramps, symptom changes, dates or hCG numbers into an answer while they wait.

**Current findings:** §§2, 5–6; especially waiting/reassurance and care navigation.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “the problem is not simply too many tabs”

**What should change:** The need is often an explanation of what is known, what is not known, what the next assessment will resolve and who is available during the interval. Repeated questions with near-identical numbers are retained as further evidence rather than treated as independent parents or prevalence. Some questions precede any loss; uncertainty must not be explained solely by prior trauma. Do not turn the parents’ interpretations of tests or symptoms into diagnostic rules.

### 4. Tests, trackers, reports, feeds and AI can offer relief and can manufacture another thing to check.

**Current findings:** §§4, 6, 10–11, 15, 16.2, 17.6.

**Relationship:** confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “More information can reassure or renew fear.”

**What should change:** Add the exact feedback mechanisms: Clearblue weeks indicators, Inito/temperature/heart-rate tracking, Oura, Dopplers, scan-report access and fridge thermometers. A device or a more detailed result can move the uncertainty instead of ending it. AI is used to interpret reports, plan announcements and substitute for inaccessible care; authors also explicitly want to stop using it. A product promise of continuous reassurance would contradict these experiences.

**Counter-evidence reproduced below:** [pbu24-0392](#pbu24-0392), [pbu24-0621](#pbu24-0621).

**Further supporting ids:** [pbu04-0034], [pbu24-0028], [pbu24-0029], [pbu24-0252], [pbu24-0564], [pbu24-0633], [pbu40-0132], [pbu40-0139], [pbu40-0219], [pbu40-0313].

### 5. Screening and fetal findings create decisions about further tests, complex care, termination, delivery and possible death.

**Current findings:** §§4–6, 9–10; add fetal-diagnosis and perinatal-loss pathways.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The decision and care experience need their own treatment.”

**What should change:** A low-risk screen can coexist with other findings or persistent uncertainty. Parents distinguish statistical reassurance from the particular result they fear, question missing twin information, and weigh invasive testing, waiting and future care. Some plan surgery/NICU; others confront TFMR or a funeral, including loss of one twin while carrying another. Hypothetical certainty can collapse at the actual scan. Keep testimony about conditions, risks and options attributed; no clinical conclusions are inferred.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

##### pbu24-0625

> So three/four months after I had my son I got pregnant, I didn’t know until several weeks into my first trimester. This was a complete surprise and also very problematic. As mentioned above I had an emergency C-section and so my stomach was cut open from hip to hip, it takes a year to 18 months to recover. My partner was heavily pushing termination as he did in the first pregnancy, I knew I’d have no emotional support. The doctors told me to hold off on abortion as the pill and the trauma to the uterus could cause my uterus to tear in half. So we waited, unfortunately I got Covid and was unable to attend my anatomy scan done until I was two weeks out from testing positive. I ended up getting my scan at 22 almost 23 weeks. They found that my daughter had a heart defect that was severe. They wanted me to have a fetal cardiologist take a look at it. Long story short she has a valve blockage so the right ventricle of her heart is not pumping any blood or receiving any. She will survive labor, she would need a surgery immediately to bypass the ventricle, the surgery at 3 to 5 months and she’d probably stay in the Nicu between those two surgeries and a third surgery from ages 3 to 5. She would also need a heart transplant anywhere from the ages tend to middle age and a potential liver transplant as pumping from the left ventricle only can cause issues with the liver having two kids with serious medical needs is very difficult and this one is much more severe plus there is a 33% chance of her dying within 18 months. I’m obviously very heartbroken, as much as I love my son and I do he’s the best kid. I have always wanted a daughter. These pregnancy has been really hard as well and part of me feels very guilty for wanting something so bad for all these years and going through all those surgeries just terminate plus the termination process is pretty graphic. They stick a needle in and stop the fetus‘s heart and then basically either surgically remove it or suck it out with a tube.  I’m genuinely torn. I don’t know if I should let nature tickets course, but also having a child being born into such hard circumstances seems very cruel. There are serious risks to me with having the abortion. The uterus could still tear and I could have massive internal bleeding, which has happened twice in my life thus far not from abortion, but labor and a different uterine surgery. I also have a heart condition and my blood pressure has been incredibly low so I’m afraid of going under anesthesia as that could have a very bad outcome as well and also becoming infertile terrifies me. also on the ethical side, I can’t imagine pushing my son to the side to deal with my daughter’s more serious medical issues. I would never wanna do that to a child and make them feel neglected. As selfish as this is, I feel like there’s something wrong with me that I keep having kids with rare disorders that they can’t explain. But that is kind of besides the point.

### 6. Wanting a child, conceiving after infertility or planning a pregnancy does not settle whether to continue this pregnancy.

**Current findings:** §§1–2, 6, 9–10; add decision-to-continue material before assuming preparation for parenting.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Trimester, pregnancy number and a calendar are not sufficient personalisation by themselves.”

**What should change:** The choice includes career, timing, autonomy, prior illness, existing children, money, relationships, personal beliefs, adoption, abortion and fear of future regret in either direction. Some are pro-choice and choose continuation; some choose termination and consider it right; some regret or change course. Neither excitement nor eventual gratitude is a prerequisite for an honest account. A partner’s attachment or detailed care plan is not the pregnant parent’s consent. No purchase intention for the proposed guide follows from any of these decisions.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

> I became pregnant by him earlier in our relationship and had a termination. It was physically painful and emotionally very difficult for me, and I can’t imagine going through another one. But now I’m pregnant again, and I’m terrified of making a permanent decision because of a relationship I’m already deeply unhappy in. Part of me keeps wondering what else is out there. What would my life look like with someone closer to my age, someone I was actually attracted to and emotionally connected with? Have I wasted two years of my twenties? > After that my pregnancy went more or less smooth until that one call from my doctor. She called me 2 weeks after a blood test.  Where she did not explain properly in the beginning why she did it. Turns out I had CMV. A virus where I did not know it even existed. From that on horrible weeks started. She briefly explained via the phone that I need to come to do another blood test. Which I could not as I was over 5 hours away from my doctor. So I tried several doctors close to where I was. After some tears I found one doing another blood test. After that I needed to arrange the communication between the doctors and the lab. I asked if I could my original doctor as I traveled back home (The other doctor gave me sick leave) I was told she is busy and I need to go to another doctor which is more specialized. I just had a few phone calls with her. And was alone …. alone with Google. Looked how other families dealt with it. Saw how other woman lost the child during late pregnancy. Asked myself if I could do it. Asked myself If I could keep up hope. The specialist was telling me I could take medication but those were not approved and I would need to take the risk and pay it myself. But it would be  not wise to end the pregnancy now. I felt restrain. I noticed how I already made my decision. In between I also had an appointment with my midwife who told me to wear a mask and not go to toilet at the doctors office anymore as I am a risk to other pregnant woman (short note the midwife working from the same place as my doctor). Long story short I ended the pregnancy. For me it is still today the right decision. As I was mentally … let’s say affected back then.

### 7. Some parents are dealing with threats, reproductive pressure, violence or control, not an unallocated household task.

**Current findings:** §§3, 5–6, 9–11, 17.1 and 17.7.

**Relationship:** extends; contradicts treating the partner remedy as sufficient.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Format helps the partner who wants to learn; it does not create the wish”

**What should change:** The earlier format limit is supported, but the added evidence goes further: pressure both to terminate and continue, contraception interference, threats of abandonment/custody, drug secrecy, restrictions on therapy, physical aggression and financial control. Include controlling families and the pregnant parent’s reported aggression as well as partner aggression. A short task list, audio chapter or joint calendar cannot explain or repair these circumstances. Preserve who says what, including fathers’ own justifications; do not substitute their preference for hers.

### 8. Severe depression, panic, suicidal thoughts and self-harm can begin during pregnancy and coexist with a wanted baby.

**Current findings:** §§2, 5–6, 9–10; expand prenatal mental-health evidence.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The restored words include panic, sadness, lack of excitement, body distress, regret and fear of not bonding or surviving birth.”

**What should change:** The new accounts name attempts, suicidal ideation, self-harm, rage, numbness, inability to perform ordinary tasks and fear of recurrence. They also show outward function—a clean home, food and childcare—coexisting with severe distress. Some expressly reject meditation, walking, yoga or generic encouragement after already trying them. Do not collapse these accounts into ordinary nerves or presume distress is resolved by an efficient checklist. Keep diagnostic labels as the author reports them.

### 9. Practical support, a good birth and a healthy baby can coexist with postpartum rage, dread, numbness and thoughts of harm.

**Current findings:** §§2, 5–6, 9–10, 17.4; extend beyond two weeks.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “A healthy baby or an outwardly successful birth does not settle what the mother experienced; relief and improvement also appear.”

**What should change:** Add explicit descriptions of rage, frightening thoughts about hurting a baby, self-harm, delayed bonding and feeling unable to remain a parent. Fathers also report postpartum depression/rage or emptiness while performing childcare. Support, protected sleep and treatment help some; they do not rule out distress, and a six-to-eight-week collapse can follow an initially manageable period. The incident requiring infant stitches remains in the account beside the father’s reported improvement, without excusing it or inferring more than stated.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

### 10. Medication continuity and the ability to function are preparation tasks before, during and after birth.

**Current findings:** §§2, 5–6, 8–10; add medication handoffs and competing capacity needs.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The leave puzzle also involves being too ill to function, medication that interferes with work, notes withheld, paid leave spent before birth, and lost pay or insurance.”

**What should change:** The problems include inadvertently stopping medication at hospital admission, vomiting essential seizure medication, loss of function after stopping ADHD/psychiatric medication, sedation conflicting with waking for feeds, withdrawal, prior reactions and fear about milk or fetal exposure. Agreement with a prescriber does not ensure a prescription is filled. Some report benefit or easier-than-feared discontinuation. These are medication experiences and questions, not prescribing guidance or established safety claims.

### 11. Fear of losing children can suppress disclosure, change care choices and make asking for help feel dangerous.

**Current findings:** §§4–6, 9–10; add child-protection, custody and disclosure fears.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The earlier contacts-and-checklists account understated loss of trust and the reluctance to ask again.”

**What should change:** These records give that reluctance a concrete consequence: fear of being judged unfit, CPS involvement, custody loss or punishment. Parents describe withholding information from therapists, delaying disclosure of rage/SI, medication-list and false-positive testing concerns, and considering severe birth pain to avoid an epidural. Preserve the account of threatened CPS involvement and the separate secondhand account of actual removal. None establishes the law, a testing policy, or that disclosing distress causes removal.

**Further supporting ids:** none; every quote assigned to this pattern is reproduced here or under the linked counter-examples.

### 12. Care navigation often means getting someone to listen, accept responsibility, share results or provide timely follow-up.

**Current findings:** §§4–6, 8–10, 16.2, 17.4.

**Relationship:** confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Care navigation includes failed referrals and follow-up, dismissive or blaming treatment, unexplained results and conflicting professionals.”

**What should change:** Add practices refusing new pregnancy patients, repeated cancellations, closure of a birth centre, retirement of a trusted clinician, weekend gaps, long-distance transfer, unavailable specialists, results withheld as “sensitive,” and specialists disagreeing about procedures. “Normal in pregnancy” can be heard as dismissal of real incapacity. A country contact page helps only if the contact answers, will see the parent, and owns the next action. Keep kind, explanatory care and parent-reported reassurance alongside failures.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

> Hi all! I mostly just need a safe space to get all of my feelings out about the past couple of days.  
> I’m currently 30 weeks pregnant, and the other day I started having very consistent contractions. I went into L&D, where they monitored me for a while and gave me fluids in hopes that the contractions would stop. Unfortunately, they didn’t. Over the course of about three hours, I went from being dilated to a 1 to a 4.  
> I live in a very small town that isn’t equipped to care for a premature baby, so I was flown to the closest hospital with a NICU. Once I got there, thankfully, the contractions started becoming more spread out and eventually stopped. After giving me steroids and monitoring me for a while, they ultimately discharged me and I was able to come back home.  
> But there are a few parts of the whole experience that I’m still struggling with.  
> The plane ride was probably one of the hardest parts. There wasn’t much I could do except sit there and think about all of the possible outcomes, and I definitely spiraled more than once.  
> Now I’m back in my hometown, about four hours away from the hospital where I would need to deliver if I went into labor again over the next couple of weeks. I’m trying to stay calm and remind myself that things are okay right now, but I feel like I panic at every little pain or weird feeling.  
> I know that some discomfort is normal at 30 weeks, but after everything that just happened, it’s really hard not to wonder, “Is this something? Is it happening again?” Every little contraction, cramp, or ache makes me stop and think about whether I should be worried.  
> I’m incredibly grateful that everything settled down and that baby is still cooking, but mentally, I’m having a much harder time moving past the experience than I expected. I think I’m still processing how quickly everything went from a normal day to being flown to another hospital because there was a real possibility that my baby could be born at 30 weeks.

**Counter-evidence reproduced below:** [pbu24-0430](#pbu24-0430).

**Further supporting ids:** [pbu04-0090], [pbu04-0139], [pbu04-0208], [pbu04-0249], [pbu04-0282], [pbu04-0284], [pbu04-0331], [pbu24-0121], [pbu24-0134], [pbu24-0144], [pbu24-0145], [pbu24-0217], [pbu24-0218], [pbu24-0219], [pbu24-0229], [pbu24-0230], [pbu24-0431], [pbu24-0433], [pbu24-0468], [pbu24-0469], [pbu24-0511], [pbu24-0512], [pbu24-0538], [pbu24-0586], [pbu40-0008], [pbu40-0021].

### 13. Trauma can concern pain, consent, handling and missing explanations even when the baby survives and preparation was extensive.

**Current findings:** §§2, 4–6, 10–11.

**Relationship:** confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “A flexible plan helps some people, but the earlier flexibility conclusion cannot explain every outcome or place responsibility for traumatic care on the woman.”

**What should change:** Specific accounts include failed spinal/epidural relief, morphine that does nothing, painful cervical checks, episiotomy/stitching felt, restraint or positioning without agreement, and silence during neonatal emergencies. A clinician separately describes staff injury, emergency haemorrhage, limited time and consent dilemmas; keep that as a professional perspective. Positive planned sections, epidurals, inductions, hospital and home births counter any conclusion that one method is inherently traumatic. The source cannot establish that a class or plan would have prevented the reported events.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

##### pbu24-0343

Recorded context: professional; birth. Clinician's account of staff injury, emergency treatment and consent; claims are attributed to this speaker.

> I’ve had two colleagues permanently injured by laboring women. I can’t fathom how difficult labor is, and what a primal head-space it would occupy. If a laboring person is hitting or grabbing me when I am trying to treat them with my hands, then I have to assume that they are not granting me consent to touch them. This is a big problem when people are hemorrhaging and we need to do that (sometimes) painful uterine massage. Hemorrhages can be fast, and uterine massage is usually the quickest, most effective treatment. I will take my hands away, explain that this is an emergency and that I may need to do some painful things to help them. Usually that effectively shifts people out of that lashing-out headspace. But sometimes, people still lash out and then it gets really complicated. Do I essentially commit battery to save their life?  Or do I wait for them to regain control while they bleed another 500cc?  It seems like some of people enter labor feeling like this behavior is culturally ok. It’s really not. Please consider setting an intention not to harm anyone in your labor. I had a colleague who needed neck surgery. Another one whose career was ended with a back injury.  I understand that with pain / the amygdala/ potential trauma response / etc, sometimes it may happen. But it seems like some women don’t have any qualms about doing it.  Thank you. Best wishes to all of you!
<!-- /voice -->

##### pbu24-0344

Recorded context: professional; birth. Clinician's account of staff injury, emergency treatment and consent; claims are attributed to this speaker.

> In general, I warn first. Sometimes, during a true emergency, my hands go from this place to that place. Honestly, I am not running a commentary of now I need to touch here, and now here, and now here…. because I’m giving orders to nurses and looking at vitals and assessing a million variables.  But I will say at the start that “ok, this has shifted into a hemorrhage, this is not a normal situation, and I may need to do some uncomfortable things to help you”.

No big deal.” So I agreed to it. She gloved up, I got into position, and without warning she was inside me and it was the worst most piercing pain I’ve ever had. I kept saying, “wait wait wait I wasn’t ready.” And was crawling up the table. She grabbed my legs with her other arm and kept going deeper and deeper not saying a single word to me the whole time. It felt like I was being shredded on the inside. It was one of the most painful things I’ve ever felt. And not once did she talk with me to assure me or even tell me she was going to go inside me when she did. I ended up laying there completely stiff and numb when she finally stopped. She said, “Yeah. You’re not even a little dilated or effaced. Your labor likely won’t progress well on its own. You have two choices. Either come in on Monday for a recheck, or schedule an induction date today. I’m not gonna make the choice for you so what do you want?” I ended up opting for coming back on Monday. But the entire experience was horrific and I’m terrified to go back.
<!-- /voice -->

I just wanted to not be in agony. They left the Jada in twice as long as they told me they were going to. Once it was out, the relief was immediate. My pain was still high, but it was finally managable.

### 14. Useful preparation must fit the actual medical situation, available time and the support contract the parent is buying.

**Current findings:** §§4, 6, 8, 10–11, 16.2, 17.4.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Preparation can support confidence or disappoint, particularly when women hear promises about pain, interventions or what their bodies can do.”

**What should change:** A medically complex or possibly premature birth can make scheduled classes inaccessible and home/unmedicated-birth teaching upsetting from the start. Self-paced, medical-birth material is an explicit request. Doula value also depends on when attendance is owed and what the refund clause excludes. Some reject paying a stranger to stand in for dead/absent family, while others want advocacy despite an otherwise good birth. Keep practical product/list requests, professional training enquiries and actual parent purchases distinct.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

### 15. The first weeks involve the mother’s recovery, feeding capacity, separation and connection, not simply supplies for a healthy baby.

**Current findings:** §§2, 4–6, 9–10, 17.4.

**Relationship:** confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Recovery is not just a supplies checklist”

**What should change:** Add readmission, infection, retained tissue, repeated repairs, inability to lift or move, delayed holding and grief for the golden hour. NICU discharge can remove the monitors that made the baby feel safe, and an early illness can keep shaping later fear. Feeding can mean six-to-seven hours of nursing plus pumping, formula changes, weight deadlines and crying after appointments; repeated practical effort is not evidence of personal failure. Improvement, pride, donor milk, shared feeding and a less demanding routine also belong here. The effective horizon extends beyond two weeks.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

> I had an even rougher delivery and first week postpartum. I ended up with 4 tears, including a tear to my cervix. I hemorrhaged, lost a quarter of my blood volume, and needed a blood transfusion. I was in the hospital for 5 days. I got 2 more blood transfusions, a 24 hr mag drip, and loads of antibiotics. I was nearly septic and my doctors told me I nearly had to have a hysterectomy. All that to say, I have a lot of trauma I'm working through. But since coming home, I've been doing really well. Healing has gone great, I've been in therapy and managing on a low dose of Zoloft.

### 16. Being practically helpful and being emotionally available are different, and fathers have their own fears and constraints.

**Current findings:** §§5–6, 8–10, 16.1, 17.1.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The gap includes practical support during pain, illness and recovery and partners’ own fear and exhaustion.”

**What should change:** Some partners clean, cook and cover shifts while the pregnant parent still feels unheard or unable to disclose fear. Other parents welcome active help, and a father wants to remain a husband rather than a remote manager across immigration separation. Work, study, military absence and leave limit presence; birth fear and relationship distress are not automatically lack of knowledge. Helpful partners, parents who love differently and distressed fathers complicate the stereotype of the uninvolved man.

### 17. Help is useful only when it respects privacy, trust, boundaries and the work the parent actually needs done.

**Current findings:** §§3, 5–6, 9–10.

**Relationship:** confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The visitor calendar alone misses bodily privacy, trust and the mother’s ability to recover.”

**What should change:** Families can cook and clean, add mess and hosting, demand a place at birth, insist on the role of an experienced parent, disparage feeding choices or use support as a condition of contact. Some helpers are unsafe because of prior abuse, rather than merely annoying. Include trustworthy support and parents who want help instead of assuming all visits should be restricted. A shower can itself be unwanted despite stable finances.

### 18. Announcing a pregnancy is a decision about grief, safety, privacy and relationships, not just the right gestational week.

**Current findings:** §§3, 5–6, 9; add a distinct announcement/disclosure subsection.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Family pressure concerns birth choice, vaccines, feeding, body size, risk, and who gets access during recovery.”

**What should change:** Add the timing and wording problem: a bereaved friend, a child who has experienced stillbirth, workplace performance, a new relationship, cultural shame, unauthorised disclosure and relatives who think they should know first. Early announcement can provide love after loss and make retraction agonising. Delaying can protect privacy and isolate the parent. Angel emojis have different meanings to different recipients. There is no single week, disclosure rule or script that suits these accounts.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

**Further supporting ids:** [pbu04-0032], [pbu04-0172], [pbu04-0271], [pbu04-0277], [pbu24-0242], [pbu24-0299], [pbu24-0541], [pbu24-0571], [pbu40-0303].

### 19. Another pregnancy changes what a parent can physically do and who is actually safe and available to care for existing children.

**Current findings:** §§2, 8–10, 16.1, 17.2–4.

**Relationship:** confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Pregnancy number alone remains too thin a selector.”

**What should change:** Add nausea preparation before conception, sensory or disability needs, inability to attend infusions without childcare, grief about a toddler losing nursing/one-to-one time, and recovery tasks that remain impossible after surgery. A manual car, school runs, distance from a stranger-averse toddler and no trusted replacement make “get help” or “make a calendar” incomplete. Existing-child capacity can affect travel, birth attendance and whether to have another baby at all.

### 21. The person and the family imagined before pregnancy can be mourned, including an imagined child’s sex or sibling relationship.

**Current findings:** §§6, 9–10; add gender-disappointment material explicitly.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The restored words include panic, sadness, lack of excitement, body distress, regret and fear of not bonding or surviving birth.”

**What should change:** Some fear being reduced to reproductive/domestic functions or losing a hard-won self, career, exercise or body. Others feel proud. Gender disappointment includes fear based on abuse, wanting a daughter or son, disappointment after the last chance, and the finality of a previous baby’s death when the next fetus is a different sex. Do not soften the stated wishes or turn them into a moral judgement. These are distinct from a generic lack of excitement and are not resolved by “healthy is all that matters.”

### 22. Wanting closeness, declining sex and fearing coercion are different needs, and pregnancy/postpartum can expose all three.

**Current findings:** §§2, 5–6, 9–10; add intimacy and reproductive-consent evidence.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The first weeks include physical incapacity, feeding pressure, conflicting instructions, loneliness, regret and delayed connection.”

**What should change:** Keep the pregnant parent who wants intimacy while respecting the partner’s refusal distinct from postpartum pressure, a missed condom, threats, prior assault and fear of retaliation against a child. Include persistent sexual pain, altered sensation, concern about being stitched too tightly, and sensory limits around breasts/feeding. Financial provision does not remove these needs; a partner task list does not address consent. The post-ectopic wish to resume sex is a question in the record, not advice to disregard care instructions.

### 23. Advice and stories about protecting the baby can turn ordinary distress, food choices or panic into another perceived failure.

**Current findings:** §§3, 6–7, 10.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “They make the emotional cost explicit instead of describing it from a distance.”

**What should change:** Authors worry that crying, arguments, grief, panic or stress itself harmed the baby or caused loss/stillbirth. Others try to meet an ideal of flawless weight gain, food safety or medication abstinence. “Do not stress” can be experienced as another impossible requirement. This is an interpretation of the accounts; the digest does not establish that the emotion or action caused the feared outcome.

**Distinctive evidence, verbatim:**

### 24. Money can determine care access, independence, relief and whether continuing a pregnancy feels possible.

**Current findings:** §§8–10, 16.3–5, 17.2 and 17.5.

**Relationship:** confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “Costs include private scans and tests, physical and mental-health care, insurance gaps, birth bills, paid support and attempts to obtain relief.”

**What should change:** The added specificity includes rent equal to earnings, no money for food/transport, maternity-debt bankruptcy, an early delivery down payment before viability, insurance classification after loss, denied leave pay, career disruption and childcare that prevents treatment. A small guide price cannot solve these constraints. The money index below includes spending without an amount, proposed purchases, refusals, refunds, coverage and professional fees, and distinguishes each from willingness to pay for Mamivio.

## Outliers and counter-examples

These cut against blanket conclusions about distress, care, preparation, birth methods, disclosure or the need for more information. Their presence does not cancel the difficult accounts.

### C1. Joy and contentment after loss are present; the next pregnancy is not always dominated by dread.

The calm forty-year-old’s confidence is an expectation about postpartum, not an observed postpartum outcome.

### C2. Previous anxiety or loss does not guarantee a difficult postpartum period.

These are affirmative accounts of a boring/normal period and reduced anxiety, not evidence against other parents’ severe symptoms.

### C3. Planned sections, pain relief, induction, hospital birth and home birth can each be described positively.

The same parent can describe several methods favourably. Their results cannot establish method superiority or product efficacy.

## What fits nowhere

These need new explicit treatment or a provenance distinction; they should not disappear into a generic planning, partner or anxiety paragraph. The other new trajectories are developed in the main patterns rather than repeated here: post-loss app/follow-up needs (pattern 1), fetal/perinatal loss planning (5), custody/disclosure fears (11), announcement decisions after grief (18), gender disappointment (21), and sexual/reproductive consent (22).

### N1. Continuing pregnancy without intending to parent, including a trans carrier considering adoption.

**Current findings:** §§1, 6, 9–10; new adoption/gestational-parent trajectory.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “She is pregnant, often for the first time”

**What should change:** The current reader description mostly assumes a mother preparing to parent. Separate pregnancy, decision to continue, intention to parent and identity. The ledger/record context explicitly identifies the American Adoptions enquirer as a first-time pregnant trans parent; the field below is retained exactly and is not a statement that an agency covers costs.

##### pbu04-0007

> I'm 38, this is my first pregnancy, I'm about 6 weeks. I have an IUD but it failed. I'm about 80% certain I don't want to parent although my partner could go either way.
<!-- /voice -->

##### pbu04-0008

> My question is it moral to continue a pregnancy knowing it will almost certainly end in adoption?
<!-- /voice -->

### N2. Paternity uncertainty, medical dating and legal parenthood are distinct from knowing how to support a partner.

**Current findings:** §§5, 8–9; new paternity/legal-parenthood topic.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “From his side, he wants to help and doesn't know how”

**What should change:** Add prenatal paternity purchases and refusal to participate, uncertainty about conception, use of medical records to question paternity, and requests about acknowledgment/birth certificates. These source accounts do not establish conception dates, biological paternity or legal rights.

### N3. Adolescent pregnancy appears in the reread evidence, including fear of parents and abortion restrictions.

**Current findings:** §9, young authors; retain as research evidence.

**Relationship:** extends research coverage.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “No minors were quoted as target customers.”

**What should change:** That sentence describes the earlier customer framing; these records include an explicitly seventeen-year-old and an explicitly sixteen-year-old. Record their words and the access/family problem without turning them into evidence about commercial targeting or a guide buyer.

### N4. A relative wants an unacknowledged baby’s existence and grief to have a place in family history.

**Current findings:** §§3, 5–6; new family remembrance/intergenerational grief topic.

**Relationship:** extends.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “The visitor calendar alone misses bodily privacy, trust and the mother’s ability to recover.”

**What should change:** The relative_caregiver record context describes an adult sibling discussing a loss at five months of pregnancy. Missing pictures, name, burial information and permission to grieve do not fit a visitor schedule or a pregnancy-product request.

### N5. Fathers can themselves describe opposing continuation or threatening departure.

**Current findings:** §§5–6, 9; expand the other-side account.

**Relationship:** extends and challenges a uniformly helpful-intent account.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “From his side, he wants to help and doesn't know how”

**What should change:** Preserve these men’s own reasoning alongside pregnant parents’ accounts of pressure. A father’s preferred relationship conditions or assertion of fairness is his perspective, not a joint decision or a rebuttal of the other parent’s autonomy.

### N6. A professional training fee and a research recruitment prompt are not purchases or lived experiences of pregnant customers.

**Current findings:** §§5, 8, 12–13; provenance and professional-side evidence.

**Relationship:** confirms.

**Current conclusion (exact passage):** “There is still no demonstrated purchase of the proposed Mamivio guide in this corpus.”

### Additional provenance that affects interpretation

The father’s rage account and infant injury are linked passages, not separate independent experiences: [pbu04-0123](#pbu04-0123), [pbu04-0124](#pbu04-0124). The clinical haemorrhage/consent explanation is a professional perspective: [pbu24-0343](#pbu24-0343), [pbu24-0344](#pbu24-0344), [pbu24-0345](#pbu24-0345). The prospective-doula enquiry includes a parent’s own birth and a professional-career request: [pbu24-0068](#pbu24-0068).

The record context says the author disclosed ChatGPT editing in [pbu40-0246](#pbu40-0246); [pbu40-0247](#pbu40-0247) supplies the financial explanation. [pbu24-0550](#pbu24-0550) reports using ChatGPT to understand a scan, while [pbu24-0635](#pbu24-0635) wants to stop torturing her mind on AI; the retained quotations are their framing, not a chatbot’s clinical explanation. [pbu40-0197](#pbu40-0197) ends without an outcome in the ledger; none is inferred.

## Money

Every identified price, purchase, proposed/refused spend, refund term, cost, coverage constraint or explicit financial tradeoff in this share is indexed below, including ordinary food/property purchases where stated. Amounts, currencies, benefits and coverage are as reported. A proposed ceiling, question or quoted fee is labelled separately from a completed purchase. Financial circumstances are included so that “no price stated” does not erase a cost barrier. Each link points to the full verbatim field above; no price or purchase of the proposed Mamivio guide appears.

The money index contains 140 quote ids. It is not a count of purchases or purchasers.

## Requests

This index captures explicit product/resource recommendations, practical plans, lists, wording, care/service requests and requests described as already made. A request for community experiences about a named product/service is retained as such; it is not a purchase commitment. Generic “has anyone felt this?” or success-story-only questions remain in the pattern evidence and are not automatically treated as product demand. Professional and third-party perspectives are identified in the labels/context.

The request index contains 165 quote ids, including multiple passages from one request; it is not a count of parents or distinct requests.

## Verification

Programmatic checks against the three original JSONL files: 885 full quote fields reproduced exactly, with no spelling, punctuation or whitespace normalisation; 1,317 mentioned ids exist in the share; zero unknown ids; zero unmentioned ids. Every exact current-conclusion passage was also required to occur literally in the current synthesis/findings.md. All money/request ids point to a reproduced full field. All records typed purchase or price_signal are in the money index.

Coverage: quotes in the share **1,317** (ur04 **356**, ur24 **635**, ur40 **326**); quotes reproduced verbatim **885 unique** (**885 reproductions**); further ids listed **432 unique**; ids not mentioned at all **0**.
